Proof Identity and Categorical Models of BV
Matteo Acclavio, Lutz Straßburger, Vladimir Zamdzhiev

Abstract
𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} -categories are a recent development that aims to give categorical semantics to proofs in the logic 𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} . However, due to the absence of a coherence theorem on one side and a well-defined notion of proof identity for 𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} on the other side, the precise relation between 𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} -categories and the logic 𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} is still not clear. To improve on this situation, we define in this paper a notion of proof identity for 𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} , based on the notion of atomic flows, which can be seen as a special form of string diagrams. Based on this notion of proof identity, we then strengthen the existing notion of 𝖡𝖵 \mathsf{BV} -category and prove that it is sound with respect to the logic.

1
Introduction

The logic
\mathsf{BV}

[
28
,
30
]
is an extension of multiplicative linear logic (
\mathsf{MLL}
) with a self-dual non-commutative connective
\mathbin{\triangleleft}
, called
seq
. It came to life because of an attempt to give a deductive proof system to
pomset logic

[
49
,
51
,
52
]
, which is defined in terms of proof nets and shares with
\mathsf{BV}
the same language for formulas. Only recently, it has been shown that the two logics are different
[
45
,
46
]
.
Nonetheless, the logic
\mathsf{BV}
has successfully been used to model sequentiality in the context of concurrency
[
12
,
34
,
33
]
and causality in the context of quantum computing
[
8
,
7
]
.
On the one hand, the syntax and proof theory of
\mathsf{BV}
is well-developed
[
30
,
58
,
28
]
, and it has a wide range of applications
[
3
,
12
,
33
]
,
but on the other hand, the semantics and possible models of
\mathsf{BV}
are not so well investigated.
This is currently changing through the increasing interest from the quantum computing community
[
54
,
56
,
55
,
42
]
and the development of the notion of
\mathsf{BV}
-category
[
8
,
31
]
.
Some non-trivial examples of
\mathsf{BV}
-categories are the
higher-order causal theories
of
[
54
]
and the
finite-dimensional operator spaces
of
[
42
]
.

A natural question to ask is whether the aforementioned
\mathsf{BV}
-categories are
sound
models of
\mathsf{BV}
.
This question is less silly than it seems, as the logic

\mathsf{BV}
and the notion of
\mathsf{BV}
-category have been investigated by essentially
disjoint communities.
The precise relation between
\mathsf{BV}
-categories and the logic
\mathsf{BV}
is not known and neither is soundness.

In this paper we want to improve on this situation and bring the two communities together by proving a soundness result: whenever two proofs are equal in
\mathsf{BV}
, then they have the same interpretation in the categorical model.
But for that, we need to answer another question: When are two
\mathsf{BV}
-proofs equal?
One of the most widely accepted notions of proof identity is via proof normalization, i.e., cut elimination.
However,
\mathsf{BV}
does not have a sequent calculus proof system
[
58
,
46
]
, but is presented in a deep inference setting. This means that cut elimination for
\mathsf{BV}
is not proved by the standard methods, but via a
splitting lemma

[
30
,
4
]
, which is hard to mimic in a categorical setting.
Furthermore, even in the cut-free case, it is not clear when two
\mathsf{BV}
-proofs should be the same, as deep inference allows for more rule permutations than a sequent system.

For this reason, we introduce in this paper a notion of
atomic flows

[
27
,
26
]
for
\mathsf{BV}
, that we use to identify proofs, i.e., two
\mathsf{BV}
proofs are the same if they are mapped to the same atomic flow. In that respect, atomic flows share properties of
proof nets

[
22
,
23
]
and
string diagrams

[
39
,
5
,
10
,
11
]
.

The next step, after having established a notion of proof identity for
\mathsf{BV}
,
is to find the right notion for a categorical model of
\mathsf{BV}
. However, it is not clear if the existing proposals for
\mathsf{BV}
-categories
[
8
,
31
]
are invariant with respect to cut elimination
and they also do not seem to match well with the notion of proof identity that we introduce here.
In order to provide a categorical semantics for
\mathsf{BV}
that is sound in the sense discussed above, we propose a new notion of
\mathsf{BV}
-category that we call
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category
.
Indeed, a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category is also a
\mathsf{BV}
-category in the sense of
[
8
,
31
]
(Theorem
18
), but the converse implications are unlikely to hold, in general.
The key idea behind the definition of a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category (Definition
17
) is the following: our atomic flows are string diagrams that are known to be the internal language of
strict compact closed categories

[
37
,
53
]
, and since the notion of proof identity is based on atomic flows, we may simply lift the coherence properties of strict compact closed categories (which are already known
[
37
]
) to the relevant diagrams of the
\mathsf{BV}
-category in which we interpret our proofs by assuming the existence of a suitable functor.

In summary, our paper makes the following contributions:

1.

We introduce atomic flows for
\mathsf{BV}
and use them to define proof equivalence. We show that the
splitting lemma

[
30
,
4
]
, which is at the core of the cut elimination procedure, preserves the atomic flows, and therefore cut elimination in
\mathsf{BV}
corresponds to
yanking
in atomic flows:

(1)

Therefore, if we impose (
1
) as an equality on atomic
flows, then atomic flows are preserved under cut elimination.
Note that this is not trivial: whereas in linear logic yanking corresponds to cut elimination
[
38
,
35
]
, this is not the case in classical logic
[
26
]
.

2.

We introduce a notion of
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category
which has a suitable functor into a strict compact closed category as part of the definition. This gives us a sufficient amount of coherence properties which we use to prove our main semantic result: if two
\mathsf{BV}
proofs are equivalent, then they have the same interpretation in the model. We also show that the strictified versions of the aforementioned examples of higher-order causal theories
[
54
]
and finite-dimensional operator spaces
[
42
]
form strong
\mathsf{BV}
-categories in our sense.

Outline of the paper.

In Section
2
, we recall the logic
\mathsf{BV}
, then, in Section
3
, we introduce atomic flows, and in Section
4
we prove that they are preserved under cut elimination in
\mathsf{BV}
. Then, in Section
5
we introduce our
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-categories
and prove their soundness for
\mathsf{BV}
in
\Cref
sec:semantics. Finally, in
\Cref
sec:concrete we discuss some concrete examples of strong
\mathsf{BV}
-categories.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\myskip}c@{\myskip}c@{\myskip}c}\hfil\qquad\quad&\hfil\qquad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.60533pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}}}\kern 10.60533pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to24.8218pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$a\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}a^{\bot}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\hfil\qquad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.9444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.36107pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\triangleleft(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D)$}}}\kern 2.36107pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to79.50613pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\triangleleft C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\triangleleft D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\\

Figure 1:
The inference rules of the proof systems
\mathsf{BV}=\{\equiv,\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow},{\mathsf{s}},{\mathsf{q}}{\downarrow}\}
and
\mathsf{SBV}=\mathsf{BV}\cup\{\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow},{\mathsf{q}}{\uparrow}\}
.

2
Preliminaries on BV

The set
\mathcal{F}=\{A,B,C,\ldots\}
of formulas is generated by a countable set of
atoms

\mathcal{A}
and a
unit

\mathbb{I}
using the following grammar:

\hfill A,B\coloneqq\mathbb{I}\mid a\mid a^{\bot}\mid(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\mid(A\triangleleft B)\mid(A\mathop{\otimes}B)\qquad\text{ with }a\in\mathcal{A}\hfill

(2)

using the binary connectives
\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}
(
par
),
\triangleleft
(
seq
), and
\mathop{\otimes}
(
tensor
). The
negation

(\cdot)^{\bot}
can be extended to all formulas via the following
De Morgan laws
:

\mathbb{I}^{\bot}=\mathbb{I}\;,\;(a^{\bot})^{\bot}=a\;,\;(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)^{\bot}=A^{\bot}\mathop{\otimes}B^{\bot}\;,\;(A\triangleleft B)^{\bot}=A^{\bot}\triangleleft B^{\bot}\;,\;(A\mathop{\otimes}B)^{\bot}=A^{\bot}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B^{\bot}

(3)

It follows that
(A^{\bot})^{\bot}=A
for all
A
. A
context
is a formula with a unique occurrence of the
hole

[\cdot]
occurring at the place of an atom:

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\;\coloneqq\;[\cdot]\;\mid\;B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\;\mid\;\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\;\mid\;B\triangleleft\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\;\mid\;\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\triangleleft B\;\mid\;B\mathop{\otimes}\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\;\mid\;\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]\mathop{\otimes}B

Then
\mathcal{C}\!\left[{A}\right]
stands for the formula obtained from the context
\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]
by replacing the hole
[\cdot]
by the formula
A
.
We also employ a
formula equivalence

\equiv
to be the smallest congruence relation generated by:

\hfill\begin{array}[]{c@{\qqquad}c@{\qqquad}r@{\qqquad}}(A\mathop{\otimes}B)\mathop{\otimes}C\equiv A\mathop{\otimes}(B\mathop{\otimes}C)\hfil\qquad\quad&A\mathop{\otimes}B\equiv B\mathop{\otimes}A\hfil\qquad\quad&A\mathop{\otimes}\mathbb{I}\equiv A\qquad\quad\\
(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C\equiv A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C)\hfil\qquad\quad&A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\equiv B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}A\hfil\qquad\quad&A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\mathbb{I}\equiv A\qquad\quad\\
(A\triangleleft B)\triangleleft C\equiv A\triangleleft(B\triangleleft C)\hfil\qquad\quad&\hfil\qquad\quad&A\triangleleft\mathbb{I}\equiv A\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\equiv\mathbb{I}\triangleleft A$\hss}\qquad\quad\end{array}\hfill

(4)

An
inference rule
is a scheme

A

\mathsf{r}\;

B

where
\mathsf{r}
is its name,
A
its
premise
and
B
its
conclusion
. A
proof system
is a set of inference rules, and the rules we use in this paper are shown in
\Cref
fig:BV. For the inference rule
\equiv
, we have the side condition that
A\equiv B
via one of the equations in (
4
) above. We write it with a dotted line to ease readability of longer derivations, and we also often condense several such steps into a single one.
We call
\mathsf{BV}
the proof system
\{\equiv,\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow},{\mathsf{s}},{\mathsf{q}}{\downarrow}\}
and the proof system
\mathsf{SBV}=\mathsf{BV}\cup\{\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow},{\mathsf{q}}{\uparrow}\}
.

To build
derivations
we use the syntax of
open deduction

[
25
]
, which allows to use the same primitives that are used to construct formulas also for constructing derivations.

Definition 1
.

We define the set
\mathcal{D}=\{\Phi,\Psi,\ldots\}
of
derivations
together with two functions
\mathsf{pr},\mathsf{cn}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{F}
(called
premise
and
conclusion
, respectively) inductively as follows:

•

For every formula
A
, we have that
{A}
is a derivation and
\mathsf{pr}({A})=\mathsf{cn}({A})=A
.

•

If
\Phi
and
\Psi
are derivations and
\odot\in\{\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbin{\varotimes}\}
, then

{{\Phi}\odot{\Psi}}
is a derivation and
\mathsf{pr}({{\Phi}\odot{\Psi}})=\mathsf{pr}({\Phi})\odot\mathsf{pr}({\Psi})
and
\mathsf{cn}({{\Phi}\odot{\Psi}})=\mathsf{cn}({\Phi})\odot\mathsf{cn}({\Psi})
.

•

If
{\Phi}
and
{\Psi}
are derivations and

A

\mathsf{r}\;

B

is an instance of an inference rule with
\mathsf{cn}({\Phi})=A
and
\mathsf{pr}({\Psi})=B
then
\textstyle{\Theta={{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}}
is a derivation and
\mathsf{pr}(\Theta)=\mathsf{pr}(\Phi)
and
\mathsf{cn}(\Theta)=\mathsf{cn}(\Psi)
.

If
\mathsf{X}
is a proof system, we often write

A

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{X}

B

to denote a derivation
\Phi
with premise
\mathsf{pr}(\Phi)=A
and conclusion
\mathsf{cn}(\Phi)=B
where all inference rules used in
\Phi
are in the proof system
\mathsf{X}
.
We may denote by

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{X}

B

a derivation in
\mathsf{X}
with premise
\mathbb{I}
, and by

A

\mathsf{r}\;

B

a derivation using only instances of the rule
\mathsf{r}
.
We also write
A\,\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{X}}\,}B
(resp.
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{X}}\,}B
) if there is a derivation in the system
\mathsf{X}
with conclusion
B
and premise
A
(resp. premise
\mathbb{I}
).

Now consider the following two rules, called
axiom
and
cut
.

\mathbb{I}

{\mathsf{i}}{\downarrow}\;

A^{\bot}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}A

and

A\mathbin{\varotimes}A^{\bot}

{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}\;

\mathbb{I}

(5)

They are the generalized form of the two inference rules
\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}
respectively.
The cut elimination in
\mathsf{BV}
is the following statement, whose proof will be discussed in
\Cref
sec:splitting.

Theorem 2
.

Let
A
be a formula. If
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{BV}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}\}}\,}A
then
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{BV}}\,}A
.

We conclude this section by recalling some basic and easy to prove properties of
\mathsf{BV}
.

Proposition 3
.

The following hold:

1.

The rule
{\mathsf{i}}{\downarrow}
is derivable in
\{\equiv,\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow},\mathsf{s},{\mathsf{q}}{\downarrow}\}=\mathsf{BV}
.

2.

The rule
{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}
is derivable in
\{\equiv,\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow},\mathsf{s},{\mathsf{q}}{\uparrow}\}=\mathsf{SBV}\setminus\{\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow},{\mathsf{q}}{\downarrow}\}
.

3.

Every rule
\mathsf{r}\mathord{\uparrow}
is derivable in
\{\equiv,{\mathsf{i}}{\downarrow},{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow},{\mathsf{s}},\mathsf{r}\mathord{\downarrow}\}
.

Proof.

The first two statements are proved by induction on
A
, and the third statement by the derivation
on the left of
\Cref
fig:dersProofs.
∎

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad\vrule\qquad}c@{\qquad\vrule\qquad}c}{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\hfil\hskip 17.00024pt\vrule\hskip 17.00024pt&{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\hfil\hskip 17.00024pt\vrule\hskip 17.00024pt&{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\end{array}

Figure 2:
Derivations to prove
\Cref
prop:i.3 and the two directions of
\Cref
prop:SBV.2

Proposition 4
.

Let
A
and
B
be formulas.
Then,

1.

\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{SBV}}\,}A
iff
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{BV}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}\}}\,}A
, and

2.

A\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{SBV}}\,}B
iff
\mathord{\vdash_{\mathsf{SBV}}\,}A^{\bot}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B
.

Proof.

The first statement follows immediately from
\Cref
prop:i.
To prove left-to-right (resp. right-to-left) implication in the second statement, we assume the existence of a derivation
\Phi
with premise
A
and conclusion
B
(resp. a derivation
\Phi^{\prime}
with premise
\mathbb{I}
and conclusion
A^{\bot}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B
) in
\mathsf{SBV}
, and we construct the derivations in the center (resp. right) of
\Cref
fig:dersProofs.
∎

3
Atomic Flows

Atomic flows
were originally introduced in
[
27
,
26
]
as a graphical formalism to study normalization properties of proofs for classical logic.
Because of their “graphical nature”, atomic flows can be seen as relatives of Girard’s
proof nets

[
22
,
16
]
and Buss’
logical flow graphs

[
48
,
13
]
. Atomic flows have never been considered for linear logic because of the existence of proof nets. However, for
\mathsf{BV}
, we do not have proof nets,
1
1

1

The proof nets of
pomset logic

[
50
,
51
]
do not have a suitable correctness criterion for
\mathsf{BV}

[
45
,
46
]
.
and therefore, atomic flows for
\mathsf{BV}
can also be seen as a first step towards the development of proof nets for
\mathsf{BV}
.

The basic idea of atomic flows is to track atom occurrences in a derivation, abstracting away from the logical structure of the formulas (i.e., the connectives and their arrangement). In that respect, they can also be seen as a special case of
string diagrams

[
5
,
10
,
11
]
, or simply
two-dimensional diagrams

[
39
]
.

We write
\mathcal{A}^{\bot}=\{a^{\bot},b^{\bot},\ldots\}
for the set of negated atoms and we let
\mathcal{Q}=\{p,q,s,t,\ldots\}
be the set of finite strings over the set
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\bot}
of
literals
.
We write
\varepsilon
for the
empty string
and
p\boxtimes q
for
string concatenation
.

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}$}\end{array}

Figure 3:
Generators (left) and equivalences (right) for atomic flows, with
a
and
b
literals.

Definition 5
.

An
atomic flow

\phi\colon p\to q
is a two-dimensional diagram written as

Ag
\phi
Ag
a_{1}
Ag
Ag
\cdots
Ag
Ag
a_{n}
Ag
b_{1}
Ag
Ag
\cdots
Ag
Ag
b_{m}

where
p=\mathsf{in}_{\phi}=a_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes a_{n}
is its
input
and
q=\mathsf{out}_{\phi}=b_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes b_{m}
its
output
, with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m}\in\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{A}^{\bot}
.

The set of atomic flows is generated from the basic flows shown on the left of
\Cref
fig:af
(the identity flow, the simple crossing, and the cap and the cups making interact dual literals)
via following two operations:

•

vertical composition:
if
\phi\colon p\to q
and
\psi\colon q\to s
are atomic flows, then so is
\theta=\phi\fatsemi\psi\colon p\to s
, depicted on the left of
\Cref
eq:afcomp below, and

•

horizontal composition:
if
\phi\colon p\to q
and
\psi\colon s\to t
are atomic flows, then so is
\theta=\phi\boxtimes\psi\colon p\boxtimes s\to q\boxtimes t
, depicted on the right of
\Cref
eq:afcomp below,

(6)

quotiented by the equivalence relation generated by
(\phi\fatsemi\phi^{\prime})\boxtimes(\psi\fatsemi\psi^{\prime})=(\phi\boxtimes\psi)\fatsemi(\phi^{\prime}\boxtimes\psi^{\prime})
and the equations shown on the right of
\Cref
fig:af.
We denote the
empty atomic flow
by
\varnothing\colon\varepsilon\to\varepsilon
.

We are now going to show how
\mathsf{SBV}
-derivations are translated into atomic flows. For this we associate to each formula
A
it string
\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}
of atom occurrences, which is formally defined as follows:

\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}\mathbb{I}\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}=\varepsilon\qquad\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}a\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}=a\qquad\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}a^{\bot}\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}=a^{\bot}\qquad\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}=\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}=\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathbin{\varotimes}B\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}=\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}\boxtimes\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}B\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}

(7)

For each formula
A
, we may denote the
identity flow

\mathsf{id}_{A}\colon\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}\to\mathopen{|\mkern-2.5mu|}A\mathclose{|\mkern-2.5mu|}
as
|\cdots|
or as
Ag
Ag
A
.
Moreover, we may also use the abbreviations in

We associate to each derivation
\Phi
in
\mathsf{SBV}
its atomic flow
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)
inductively as follows:

•

For a formula
A
, we have
\mathsf{AF}\left(A\right)=\mathsf{id}_{A}
, in particular,
\mathsf{AF}\left(\mathbb{I}\right)=\varnothing
;

•

for
\odot\in\{\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbin{\varotimes}\}
, we define
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\odot\Psi\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\boxtimes\mathsf{AF}\left(\Psi\right)
.

•

For
\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{SBV}
, and
\Theta={}\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}
then
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Theta\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\fatsemi\mathsf{AF}\left(\mathsf{r}\right)\fatsemi\mathsf{AF}\left(\Psi\right)
, where
\mathsf{AF}\left(\mathsf{r}\right)
is defined as above.

(10)

Proposition 8
.

Yanking is terminating and confluent.

Proof.

Termination is immediate because the atomic flow gets smaller at each step, and confluence follows because the only critical pairs are given by the two ways of reducing

Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag

to

Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag

and the two ways of reducing

Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag

to

Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag

.
∎

This means that each atomic flow
\phi
has a unique normal form under
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Y}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}
, and we denote this normal form by
\mathsf{Y}\left(\phi\right)
and call it the
yanking of
\phi
.

4
Cut Elimination is Yanking. Also in BV.

Cut elimination in a deep inference system usually means that the
up-fragment
(i.e., the rules with the
\uparrow
in the name) can be eliminated. Propositions
3
and
4
above say that
\mathsf{BV}
also follows this pattern. Cut elimination in
\mathsf{BV}
is proved via a
splitting lemma
that allows to decompose a derivation of the premise of an up-rule into a derivation of the conclusion. This
splitting
(proved for
\mathsf{BV}
in
[
30
]
; see also
[
29
,
33
]
for alternative presentations) is a global property of a derivation and provides,
a priori
, no information about the atomic flows. But in order to establish the relation between cut elimination and yanking in
\mathsf{BV}
, which is a local rewrite rule, we need to strengthen the splitting lemma to also talk about the atomic flows.

Lemma 9

(Splitting, improved formulation)
.

Let
A
,
B
, and
K
be formulas, and let
a
be an atom.

1.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

(A\mathbin{\varotimes}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K

, then there are formulas
K_{A}
and
K_{B}
and derivations

{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{ and }{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{ and }{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{, s.t. }\mathsf{AF}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)

2.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K

, then there are formulas
K_{A}
and
K_{B}
and derivations

{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{ and }{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{ and }{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{, s.t. }\mathsf{AF}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)

3.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

a\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K

, then there is a formula
K
and a derivation

{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{, s.t. }\mathsf{AF}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)

Proof.

The proof is the same as for the standard splitting lemma for
\mathsf{BV}

[
30
,
57
]
, which proceeds by induction on the size of
\Phi
and a case analysis on the bottommost rule instance in
\Phi
. The only difference here is the additional observation that each transformation preserves the atomic flow. More details are in Appendix
A
.
∎

In the next step, the context
[\cdot]\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K
of the splitting lemma is generalized to an arbitrary context
\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]
.

Lemma 10

(Context Reduction, improved formulation)
.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{A}\right]

, then there is a formula
K

and derivations

{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{ for every formula $X$, such that\/ }\mathsf{AF}\left({{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)

Proof.

As in the previous lemma, the proof is a straightforward adaptation of the standard context reduction lemma for
\mathsf{BV}

[
30
,
57
]
, which proceeds by induction on the structure of
\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\cdot}\right]
, by repeatedly applying splitting, until the base case
[\cdot]\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K
is reached. It follows immediately that each transformation preserves the atomic flow.
∎

These two lemmas are enough to eliminate the rules
{\mathsf{q}}{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}
from a derivation. Again, this has already been shown in
[
30
,
57
]
, and we only need to observe what happens to the atomic flows under this transformation.

Lemma 11
.

If there is a derivation

\Phi={{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}

then there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi^{\ast}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{(A\mathbin{\varotimes}C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\mathbin{\varotimes}D)}\right]

such that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\ast}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)
.

Proof.

By the previous two lemmas, we have derivations

{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\;,\;{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\;,\;{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\;,\;{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\;,\;{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}

Which can be put together to get a derivation
\Phi^{\ast}
.
The fact that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\ast}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)
follows from the fact that atomic flows are equivalent modulo the identities in
\Cref
fig:af. More details are in Appendix
A
.
∎

Lemma 12
.

If there is a derivation

\Phi={}\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}

then there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi^{\ast}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\mathbb{I}}\right]

such that

\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Y}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\ast}\right)
.

Proof.

By context reduction and splitting, we get two formulas
K_{a}
and
K_{a^{\bot}}
and derivations

(a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{a}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{a^{\bot}}

\scriptstyle\Phi_{\mathcal{C}\!\left[{a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot}}\right]}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot}}\right]

and

\mathbb{I}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{a}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{a^{\bot}}

\scriptstyle\Phi_{\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\mathbb{I}}\right]}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{\mathbb{I}}\right]

and

a^{\bot}

\scriptstyle\Phi_{a}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

K_{a}

and

a

\scriptstyle\Phi_{a^{\bot}}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

K_{a^{\bot}}

We can then build the derivations
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\ast}
shown below.

\Phi^{\prime}={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\qquad\Phi^{\ast}={{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}

We have
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\;=\;\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)
and

\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\ast}\right)=\hbox to96.67pt{\vbox to57.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-37.83301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

by
\Cref
lem:splitting,lem:context, and

\mathsf{AF}\left({{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)\;=\;\mathsf{AF}\left({{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)

by rewriting via the equalities in
\Cref
fig:af. Therefore

\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)
is as shown on the left below. This rewrites with a single
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Y}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}
step, into the atomic flow on the right below, which is equal to
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\ast}\right)
.

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}
{}{{}}{}
