∎

Now
\Cref
thm:cutelim follows immediately from the previous two lemmas and
\Cref
prop:SBV.
However, with atomic flows, we have a stronger result.

Notation 13
.

Let
\Phi
be an
\mathsf{SBV}
derivation with premise
\mathbb{I}
. We write
\Phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{CE}}}{{\leadsto}}\Phi^{\prime}
if
\Phi^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{BV}
derivation that is obtained from
\Phi
by eliminating the
{\mathsf{q}}{\uparrow}
- and
\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}
-rule instances according to the proofs of Lemmas
11
and
12
.

We can now state and prove our main result.

Theorem 14
.

If
\Phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{CE}}}{{\leadsto}}\Phi^{\prime}
then
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right)
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
11
and
12
, we have
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{Y}}}{{\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)
. Since
\Phi^{\prime}
has no
\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}
-instance (and no
{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}
-instance), we have that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)
has no yanking redex. Hence
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right)
.
∎

Corollary 15
.

Let
\Phi
be a derivation of
A
in
\mathsf{BV}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}\}
.
Then, there is a derivation
\Phi^{\prime}
of
A
in
\mathsf{BV}
such that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right)
.

Proof.

By
\Cref
prop:i, we can construct a derivation
\Phi^{\prime}
by replacing each
{\mathsf{i}}{\uparrow}
-instance in
\Phi
by a derivation in
\{\equiv,\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow},\mathsf{s},{\mathsf{q}}{\uparrow}\}
.
By
\Cref
prop:AF-ID the atomic flow of
\Phi^{\prime}
is the same as the one of
\Phi
.
Finally, by
\Cref
thm:YAF, we can eliminate the up-rules from
\Phi^{\prime}
obtaining a derivation
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathsf{BV}
such that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right)
.
∎

We can now formally define proof identity in
\mathsf{BV}
.

Definition 16
.

Let
\Phi
and
\Psi
be two derivations of
A
in
\mathsf{SBV}
.
We say that
\Phi
and
\Psi
are
equivalent
(denoted
\Phi\sim\Psi
) if there are derivations
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Psi^{\prime}
such that
\Phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{CE}}}{{\leadsto}}\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{CE}}}{{\leadsto}}\Psi^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Psi^{\prime}\right)
.

5
What is a BV-Category?

It is commonly expected from a categorical model that it identifies proofs that
are considered equal by the logic. A typical example are
*
-autonomous
categories that identify proofs of multiplicative linear logic (
\mathsf{MLL}
) up to
rule permutations in the sequent calculus
[
9
,
40
,
35
]
.

Recall that if
\langle\mathbf{C},\otimes,\mathbf{1},\cdot^{\bot}\rangle
is a
*
-autonomous category, then
we can define another symmetric monoidal structure
A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\triangleq(A^{\bot}\otimes B^{\bot})^{\bot}
with unit
\bot\triangleq\mathbf{1}^{\bot}
by duality.
As
\mathsf{BV}
is a conservative extension of
\mathsf{MLL}
(with
\mathsf{mix}
and

\mathsf{mix_{0}}
)
2
2

2

\mathsf{mix}
is
\bot\multimap\mathbf{1}
and
\mathsf{mix_{0}}
is
\mathbf{1}\multimap\bot
. See
[
21
,
1
]
.
, it immediately follows that any categorical
model of
\mathsf{BV}
should be a
*
-autonomous category that is also isomix (this allows us to identify the two units
\mathbf{1}
and
\bot
). Following the notation of the previous sections, we denote
this unit by
\mathbb{I}
. Furthermore, since
\mathsf{BV}
has an additional
non-commutative binary connective
\mathbin{\triangleleft}
(with unit
\mathbb{I}
), we also use an
additional monoidal structure
\langle\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbb{I}\rangle
.

The first proposal for what a
\mathsf{BV}
-category should be was made in

[
8
]
followed by another one in
[
31
]
. We invite
the reader to consult Appendix
B
for the definition of a

\mathsf{BV}
-category with negation
in the sense of
[
8
]
.
The definition of a
\mathsf{BV}
-category with negation in
[
8
]

is equivalent to the definition of a
\mathsf{BV}
-category in
[
31
]
3
3

3

James Hefford, personal communication.
and henceforth
we often refer to both of these (equivalent) concepts as
\mathsf{BV}
-category
for simplicity.
The presentation of
\mathsf{BV}
-categories in
[
8
]
is based on linearly distributive categories whereas
the presentation in
[
31
]
is based on
*
-autonomous categories.

Unfortunately, soundness has not been demonstrated for the two aforementioned
proposals for
\mathsf{BV}
-categories. Furthermore, we conjecture that the notion of
proof identity, based on atomic flows, that we consider in this paper does not
match well with these proposals. Note that atomic flows in the logic
\mathsf{BV}
correspond
to the string diagrams of a strict compact closed category (see previous
section). The
\mathsf{BV}
-categories of

[
8
,
31
]
are not built around compact closed
categories and we conjecture that, in general, arbitrary
\mathsf{BV}
-categories in the sense of
[
8
,
31
]
cannot be embedded
in a compact closed category in the way we need (Definition
17
).

Because of this, we believe that our notion of proof
equivalence does not match well with the
\mathsf{BV}
-categories of

[
8
,
31
]
. This is our justification for introducing a
new notion of
\mathsf{BV}
-category (Definition
17
) for which we prove
soundness (Theorem
23
) and which behaves well with respect to
our notion of proof equivalence based on atomic flows
(
\cref
cor:proof-equivalence). We give our proposal the name
strong

\mathsf{BV}
-category
, because every strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category is also a
\mathsf{BV}
-category in the sense of
[
8
,
31
]
(Theorem

18
), but we conjecture that the converse implication
does not hold.

Before we formulate our main definition, recall that every compact closed category
(\mathbf{D},\boxtimes,J,\cdot^{\bot})
is
*
-autonomous with
A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B\cong A\boxtimes B.

In this paper we work with
strict
compact closed categories. By this we mean that:
(1) the symmetric monoidal structure is strict so that
A\boxtimes(B\boxtimes C)=(A\boxtimes B)\boxtimes C
and
J\boxtimes A=A=A\boxtimes J
;
(2) the canonical De Morgan isomorphism is the identity so that
(A\boxtimes B)^{\perp}=A^{\perp}\boxtimes B^{\perp}
;
(3) the double dual isomorphism is the identity so that
A=A^{\perp\perp}.

We also recall that a strict
*
-autonomous functor
U\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}

is a functor that preserves all of the
*
-autonomous structure up to equality, i.e.,
U(A\otimes B)=UA\otimes UB
,
U(A^{\bot})=(UA)^{\bot}
,

U(f\otimes g)=Uf\otimes Ug
, etc.

Definition 17
.

A
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category
is a tuple
\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbin{\varotimes},\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbb{I},\cdot^{\bot},U\rangle
such that:

1.

\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbin{\varotimes},\mathbb{I},\cdot^{\bot}\rangle
is a
*
-autonomous category together with an isomix isomorphism
\mathbb{I}^{\bot}\cong\mathbb{I}
;

2.

\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbb{I}\rangle
is a monoidal category;

3.

there is a natural isomorphism
\kappa_{AB}\colon A^{\bot}\mathbin{\triangleleft}B^{\bot}\cong(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)^{\bot}
;

4.

there is a natural transformation
q_{ABCD}\colon(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D)\to(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)
;

5.

U\colon\mathbf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{D}
is a faithful strict
*
-autonomous functor into a strict compact closed category
(\mathbf{D},\boxtimes,\cdot^{\bot},J)
such that:

•

U
is strict monoidal with respect to
\mathbin{\triangleleft}
;

•

U(\kappa_{AB})=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, where
X=(UA)^{\bot}\boxtimes(UB)^{\bot}=(UA\boxtimes UB)^{\bot}
in
\mathbf{D}
;

•

the following diagram commutes for any choice of objects
A,B,C,D
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U((A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U((A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Uq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}

(11)

where
\sigma
stands for the monoidal symmetry.

By using the canonical De Morgan isomorphisms in the
*
-autonomous category
\mathbf{C}
, the double isomorphism, and the self-duality of
\triangleleft
via the
\kappa
natural isomorphism, one may now define a
natural transformation

q^{\prime}_{ABCD}\colon(A\triangleleft B)\mathop{\otimes}(C\triangleleft D)\xrightarrow{\cong\circ q^{\bot}\circ\cong}(A\mathop{\otimes}C)\triangleleft(B\mathop{\otimes}D)

which is therefore principally determined by
q
via duality.
The importance of condition
5
from the above definition is
that it may be understood as specifying a coherence property. Note that
coherence for compact closed categories is already known, so the faithfulness
of
U
and the fact that
U
preserves all the relevant structure provide us
with a simple criterion to check if a diagram in a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category commutes. Less formally, condition
5
allows us to inherit many of the coherence
properties of the compact closed category
\mathbf{D}
(which are already known) into

\mathbf{C}
.

Theorem 18
.

Let
\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbin{\varotimes},\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbb{I},\cdot^{\bot},U\rangle
be a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category. Then
\langle\mathbf{C},\mathbin{\varotimes},\mathbin{\triangleleft},\mathbb{I},\cdot^{\bot},U\rangle
is also a
\mathsf{BV}
-category (with negation) in the sense of
[
8
]
and
[
31
]
.

Proof.

(Sketch.)
The proof follows easily from the fact that
U
is faithful and strictly
preserves all the relevant data which allows us to reduce the commutativity
of all the relevant diagrams in
\mathbf{C}
to their commutativity in
\mathbf{D}
under

U
. The latter is very easy to verify using the string diagram language (or
coherence theorem) for strict compact closed categories.

To illustrate this, let us show why diagram (
21
) from Appendix
B
commutes in
\mathbf{C}.

Since
U
is faithful and strictly preserves the two monoidal structures
\mathop{\otimes}
and
\triangleleft
, it follows that (
21
), i.e.,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\triangleleft B)\otimes(C\triangleleft D))\otimes(E\triangleleft F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\triangleleft B)\otimes((C\triangleleft D)\otimes(E\triangleleft F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\otimes C)\triangleleft(B\otimes D))\otimes(E\triangleleft F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\triangleleft B)\otimes((C\otimes E)\triangleleft(D\otimes F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\otimes C)\otimes E)\triangleleft((B\otimes D)\otimes F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes(C\otimes E))\triangleleft(B\otimes(D\otimes F))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\triangleleft\alpha}

commutes in
\mathbf{C}
, iff the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UD\boxtimes UE\boxtimes UF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UD\boxtimes UE\boxtimes UF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UD\boxtimes UE\boxtimes UF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UE\boxtimes UD\boxtimes UF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UE\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UD\boxtimes UF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UA\boxtimes UC\boxtimes UE\boxtimes UB\boxtimes UD\boxtimes UF}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

commutes in
\mathbf{D}
(which we recall is
strict
compact closed) and this follows immediately from the coherence theorem of symmetric monoidal categories.

The remaining diagrams can be checked in a similar way.
∎

Note that the definition of strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category requires checking considerably fewer coherence
diagrams compared to that of
\mathsf{BV}
-category (see Appendix
B
). This is largely thanks
to condition
5
, which is indeed a strong assumption.

6
Denotational Semantics

We now describe the denotational semantics of
\mathsf{BV}
in an arbitrary, but fixed,
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category with
U\colon\mathbf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{D}
the forgetful
inclusion. Because of Definition
17
.
1
, we may
choose
\mathbb{I}
to be the monoidal unit for the
\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}
monoidal structure as
well and then we can define
(\cdot)^{\bot}=(\cdot)\multimap\mathbb{I}
.
We do so in the sequel for simplicity. A
\mathsf{BV}
-formula
A
is interpreted as an object
{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\in\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{C})
as follows:

\hfill{\left\llbracket\mathbb{I}\right\rrbracket}=\mathbb{I}\qquad{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}=c_{a}\qquad{\left\llbracket a^{\bot}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\qquad{\left\llbracket A\odot B\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\odot{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}\hfill

(12)

where
c_{a}
is some chosen object of
\mathbf{C}
, and
\odot\in\{\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},\triangleleft,\mathop{\otimes}\}
.
A
\mathsf{BV}
-formula
A
also admits an interpretation
{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\in\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{D})
which can be defined by interpreting all tensors as
\boxtimes
:

\hfill{\llparenthesis\mathbb{I}\rrparenthesis}=J\qquad{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}=Uc_{a}\qquad{\llparenthesis a^{\bot}\rrparenthesis}={\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}^{\bot}\qquad{\llparenthesis A\odot B\rrparenthesis}={\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\hfill

(13)

where again
\odot\in\{\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},\triangleleft,\mathop{\otimes}\}
.
It now immediately follows that
U{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}={\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis},
because the functor
U
strictly preserves all this data.
The interpretation of formula equivalence
A\equiv B
is given by a (symmetric) monoidal natural isomorphism
{\left\llbracket A\equiv B\right\rrbracket}\colon{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\cong{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}
in
\mathbf{C}

defined in the obvious way. Likewise, formula equivalence can be interpreted in
\mathbf{D}
as a symmetric monoidal natural isomorphism
{\llparenthesis A\equiv B\rrparenthesis}\colon{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\cong{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}
in
\mathbf{D}
. Note that
in
\mathbf{D}
, all the isomorphisms are identities, except for the two symmetries of
\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}
and
\mathop{\otimes}.

Definition 19
.

Let
\Phi={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}
be a derivation in
\mathsf{SBV}
with premise
A
and conclusion
B
.
The interpretation of
\Phi
in
\mathbf{C}
is the morphism
{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}\colon{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\to{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}
defined as follows:

{\left\llbracket\Phi\odot\Psi\right\rrbracket}\coloneqq{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}\odot{\left\llbracket\Psi\right\rrbracket}\qquad{\left\llbracket{}\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}\right\rrbracket}\coloneqq{\left\llbracket\Psi\right\rrbracket}\circ{\left\llbracket\mathsf{r}\right\rrbracket}\circ{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}\qquad{\left\llbracket{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\;\;A\;\;$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to18.61086pt{\leaders{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{\kern-0.35pt$.$\kern-0.35pt}\vss}}{\hskip 18.61086pt}}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 5.26202pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.9566pt\hbox{$B$}\kern 0.9566pt$}}\kern 5.26202pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\rrbracket}\coloneqq{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\xrightarrow{{\left\llbracket A\equiv B\right\rrbracket}}{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}

where
{\left\llbracket\mathsf{r}\right\rrbracket}
is defined as follows

\hskip-8.00003pt\begin{array}[]{l}{\left\llbracket{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.60533pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}}}\kern 10.60533pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to24.8218pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$a\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}a^{\bot}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\rrbracket}\;\coloneqq\;\mathbb{I}\xrightarrow{\Lambda(\mathrm{ev}\circ\sigma\circ l)}\Big({\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\otimes{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\perp\perp}\Big)^{\bot}\quad\qquad\left(\text{where $\Big({\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\otimes{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\perp\perp}\Big)^{\bot}={\left\llbracket a\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}a^{\bot}\right\rrbracket}$ by definition}\right)\\[15.0pt]
{\left\llbracket{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to25.0996pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.74423pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 3.19444pt\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}\kern 3.19444pt$}}\kern 10.74423pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\rrbracket}\;\coloneqq\;\Big({\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}\otimes{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\Big)\xrightarrow{\sigma}\Big({\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\otimes{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}\Big)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}\mathbb{I}\\[15.0pt]
{\left\llbracket{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.61104pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\mathop{\otimes}(B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{s}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to46.50491pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathop{\otimes}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\right\rrbracket}\;\coloneqq\;\Big({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}({\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket})\Big)\xrightarrow{\Lambda(\mathrm{ev}\circ(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{ev})\circ\cong)}\Big(({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket})^{\bot}\multimap{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket}\Big)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Big(({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket}\Big)\\[15.0pt]
{\left\llbracket{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.9444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.36107pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\triangleleft(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D)$}}}\kern 2.36107pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to79.50613pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\triangleleft C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\triangleleft D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\rrbracket}\;\coloneqq\;\Big(({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket})\triangleleft({\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket})\Big)\xrightarrow{q}\Big(({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\triangleleft{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket})\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}({\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}\triangleleft{\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket})\Big)\\[15.0pt]
{\left\llbracket{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.9444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(A\triangleleft B)\mathop{\otimes}(C\triangleleft D)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to79.78394pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 2.22217pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathop{\otimes}C)\triangleleft(B\mathop{\otimes}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 2.22217pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\rrbracket}\;\coloneqq\;\Big(({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\triangleleft{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket})\mathop{\otimes}({\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket}\triangleleft{\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket})\Big)\xrightarrow{q^{\prime}}\Big(({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket})\triangleleft({\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}{\left\llbracket D\right\rrbracket})\Big)\end{array}

(14)

where
\odot\in\{\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},\triangleleft,\mathop{\otimes}\}
;

\Lambda_{ABC}\colon\mathbf{C}(A\otimes B,C)\cong\mathbf{C}(A,B\multimap C)
is the usual natural bijection;

\mathrm{ev}_{AB}\colon(A\multimap B)\otimes A\to B
is the evaluation morphism in
\mathbf{C}
;

l_{A}\colon\mathbb{I}\otimes A\cong A
is the left unitor;
the left unnamed isomorphism in the
\mathsf{s}
rule has type
({\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\multimap{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket})\mathop{\otimes}({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\multimap{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}^{\bot})\mathop{\otimes}{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\cong{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}({\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket})\mathop{\otimes}({\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}\mathop{\otimes}{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket})^{\bot}

and it is constructed using the obvious isomorphisms from the
*
-autonomous structure.

The interpretation of
\Phi
in
\mathbf{D}
is the morphism
{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}\colon{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\to{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}
defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\llparenthesis\Phi\odot\Psi\rrparenthesis}\coloneqq{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis\Psi\rrparenthesis}\qquad\left(\!\left|{}\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq{\llparenthesis\Psi\rrparenthesis}\circ{\llparenthesis r\rrparenthesis}\circ{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}\qquad\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.55551pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\;\;A\;\;$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\equiv\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to18.61086pt{\leaders{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{\kern-0.35pt$.$\kern-0.35pt}\vss}}{\hskip 18.61086pt}}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 5.26202pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.9566pt\hbox{$B$}\kern 0.9566pt$}}\kern 5.26202pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\xrightarrow{{\llparenthesis A\equiv B\rrparenthesis}}{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\\[15.0pt]
\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.60533pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}}}\kern 10.60533pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to24.8218pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$a\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}a^{\bot}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq J\xrightarrow{\eta_{{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}^{\perp}}}{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}^{\bot}\qquad\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to25.0996pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.74423pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 3.19444pt\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}\kern 3.19444pt$}}\kern 10.74423pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}^{\bot}\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{{\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis}}}J\\[15.0pt]
\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.61104pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\mathop{\otimes}(B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{s}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to46.50491pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathop{\otimes}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis C\rrparenthesis}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis C\rrparenthesis}\\[15.0pt]
\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.9444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.36107pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\triangleleft(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D)$}}}\kern 2.36107pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to79.50613pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\triangleleft C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\triangleleft D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis C\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis D\rrparenthesis}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}\left(\!\left|A\right|\!\right)\boxtimes{\llparenthesis C\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis D\rrparenthesis}\\[15.0pt]
\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.9444pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$(A\triangleleft B)\mathop{\otimes}(C\triangleleft D)$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to79.78394pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 2.22217pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$(A\mathop{\otimes}C)\triangleleft(B\mathop{\otimes}D)$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 2.22217pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)\coloneqq{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis C\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis D\rrparenthesis}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}}{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis C\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}\boxtimes{\llparenthesis D\rrparenthesis}\\[15.0pt]
\end{array}

(15)

where
\odot\in\{\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},\triangleleft,\mathop{\otimes}\}
, and where
\eta_{A}\colon J\to A^{\perp}\boxtimes A
and
\epsilon_{A}\colon A\boxtimes A^{\perp}\to J
are the unit and counit of the compact closed structure.

Remark 20
.

The definition of
{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}
can be seen as directly interpreting the atomic flow of
\Phi
as a string diagram representing the morphism in a strict compact closed category
\mathbf{D}
,
where

Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
represents the morphism
\eta
and

Ag
Ag
Ag
Ag
represents the morphism
\epsilon
, which are usually drawn as cups
and caps
, respectively, in string diagrams (see, e.g.,
[
11
]
).

The relationship between the two semantic interpretations is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 21
.

For any

\mathsf{SBV}
-derivation

\Phi={}\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}}$}}
, the following diagram commutes in
\mathbf{D}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}}

(16)

Proof.

This follows by induction on
\Phi
. The two most interesting cases are
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}
. The case for
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}
follows immediately from the definition of a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.
The case for
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}
follows easily, because
U{\left\llbracket\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}\right\rrbracket}=U(\cong)\circ U({\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket}^{\perp})\circ U(\cong)=\mathrm{id}\circ(\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma^{\perp}\boxtimes\mathrm{id})\circ\mathrm{id}=\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}={\llparenthesis\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}\rrparenthesis}

where we used the fact that
U
strictly preserves all the relevant structure and maps the two unnamed isomorphisms to identities in
\mathbf{D}
.
The remaining cases are straightforward and follow easily from standard results about
*
-autonomous and compact closed categories. More details can be found in Appendix
C
.
∎

Proposition 22
.

\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be two
\mathsf{SBV}
-derivations
such that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right).

Then
{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}={\llparenthesis\Phi^{\prime}\rrparenthesis}.

Proof.

This follows easily from
\cref
rem:af-semantics, because the semantic interpretation in the strict compact closed category
\mathbf{D}
is sound (i.e., invariant) with respect to yanking.
∎

Theorem 23

(Soundness)
.

Let
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be derivations with
\Phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{CE}}}{{\leadsto}}\Phi^{\prime}
. Then
{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket\Phi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket}
.

Proof.

Since
U
is a faithful functor, it suffices to prove that
U{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}=U{\left\llbracket\Phi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket}.

By Lemma
21
, it suffices to prove that
{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}={\llparenthesis\Phi^{\prime}\rrparenthesis}
.
Using Theorem
14
, we know that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right)
and Proposition
22

concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 24
.

If
\Phi\sim\Psi
, then
{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket\Psi\right\rrbracket},
i.e., the semantic interpretation is invariant with respect to proof equivalence.

Proof.

By the previous theorem, it suffices to prove this for normalized proofs. Since
U
is faithful, it suffices to prove

U{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}={\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}={\llparenthesis\Psi\rrparenthesis}=U{\left\llbracket\Psi\right\rrbracket}.
This now follows trivially from Proposition
22
.
∎

7
Concrete Models of BV

In this section we describe several categories that have the structure of a
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category, thus providing concrete models of the logic
\mathsf{BV}
. All the
concrete models that we consider are based on existing categories that have
already been studied in prior work. Because of the strictness requirement of
Definition
17
, a common pattern in three of the models that we
consider is that we take the
skeleton
subcategory of a category that has
already been considered in the literature. This allows us to easily satisfy the
strictness requirement. However, we conjecture
that the strictness requirement is not necessary – see Section

8
for more discussion related to this.

The most obvious, but rather degenerate, class of models is given by strict
compact closed categories (Subsection
7.1
) where one can interpret
all three multiplicative connectives of
\mathsf{BV}
using the same monoidal tensor. The
most mathematically interesting and natural example is based on
finite-dimensional operator spaces (Subsection
7.2
), with strong
links to quantum theory, where mathematicians have identified three different
operator space tensor products that allow us to interpret
\mathsf{BV}
. Our two remaining
examples are based on gluing and orthogonality
[
36
]
: the Caus[-]
construction on a category of completely positive maps (Subsection

7.3
) and finite-dimensional probabilistic coherence spaces
(Subsection
7.4
). The former has strong links to quantum
theory whereas the latter is relevant to classical probabilistic computation.

7.1
Strict Compact Closed Categories

Every strict compact closed category can be seen as a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category by taking
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{D}
and
U
to be the identity functor.

A relevant example is the
skeleton subcategory of finite-dimensional vector spaces

\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}}
whose
objects are vector spaces of the form
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
morphisms
\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{m})
are
m\times n
complex matrices,
where the multiplication of matrices serves as composition of morphisms,
the tensor product
\boxtimes
is the Kronecker product of matrices (with unit
\mathbb{C}
), and
where duality is defined as transposition of matrices, denoted
\cdot^{\mathrm{t}}
.
Note that
\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}}\simeq\mathbf{FdVect}
, because every
m\times n
matrix
determines a linear map
\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
(with respect to the
standard basis), so we may view the morphisms as linear maps as well.

Theorem 25
.

The tuple
\langle\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}},\boxtimes,\boxtimes,\mathbb{C},\cdot^{\mathrm{t}},\mathsf{Id}\rangle
is a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.

Proof.

It is well-known that
\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}}
is
strict
compact closed. See for example
[
32
]
.
∎

7.2
Finite-dimensional Operator Spaces

A
finite-dimensional operator space

[
17
]
is a pair
\left(X,\mathcal{O}\right)
consisting of
a finite-dimensional vector space
X
and
an
operator space structure

\mathcal{O}=\left\{\norm{\cdot}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}(X)}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
given by a sequence of norms
\norm{\cdot}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}(X)}\colon\mathbb{M}_{n}(X)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
on the vector space of
n\times n
matrices with entries in
X
that satisfy specific axioms (omitted here).
If
(X,\mathcal{O})
and
(Y,\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
are two operator spaces,
we say that a linear map
f\colon X\to Y
is a
complete contraction

[
17
]

if
\norm{f (A)}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}(Y)}\leq\norm{A}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}(X)}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
A\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(X)
, where by
f(A)
we denote the matrix obtained by applying
f
to each entry of
A
.

The category
\mathbf{FdOS}
of finite-dimensional operator spaces and (linear) complete contractions was recently studied in
[
42
]
, where it was shown that it is a
\mathsf{BV}
-category.
Duals (in the categorical/logical sense) coincide with the operator space duals
[
17
]
, written
\cdot^{*}
in
\mathbf{FdOS}
. The
projective tensor

\ptimes

[
17
, §7.1]
serves as the multiplicative conjunction
\mathop{\otimes}
,
the
injective tensor

\itimes

[
6
, (1.5.1)]
can be identified with the multiplicative disjunction
\mathbin{\bindnasrepma},

and the
Haagerup tensor

\hagertimes

[
17
, §9]
,
[
6
, pp. 30–34]
,
[
47
, §5]
as the seq
\triangleleft
of
\mathsf{BV}
. By
[
18
, Theorem 6.1]
(see also
[
42
]
), we can define a complete contraction

\hfill q_{ABCD}\colon(A\itimes B)\hagertimes(C\itimes D)\to(A\hagertimes C)\itimes(B\hagertimes D)\hfill

which gives us the
q
natural transformation. It is well-known that the Haagerup tensor is not symmetric and it is self-dual in
\mathbf{FdOS}

[
17
, §9]
,
thus giving us
\kappa_{AB}\colon A^{*}\hagertimes B^{*}\cong(A\hagertimes B)^{*}
.

The
skeleton subcategory

\mathbf{FdOS_{sk}}
is the subcategory of
\mathbf{FdOS}
whose objects are operator spaces of the form
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathcal{O})
for some operator space structure
\mathcal{O}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}

and whose morphisms are the complex matrices that represent linear complete contractions between such spaces. All of the aforementioned constructions can be easily adapted to
\mathbf{FdOS_{sk}}
and the obvious
forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{FdOS_{sk}}\to\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}}
strictly preserves all this data.

Theorem 26
.

The tuple
\langle\mathbf{FdOS_{sk}},\ptimes,\hagertimes,\mathbb{C},\cdot^{*},U\rangle
is a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.

Proof.

Straightforward verification by using (the adapted) results from
[
42
]
.
∎

The theory of operator spaces has strong links to quantum theory. Von Neumann
algebras (e.g.,
B(H)
– the algebra of bounded operators on a Hilbert space

H
) are operator spaces and so are their preduals (e.g.,
T(H)
– the trace
class operators on a Hilbert space
H
). The former can be used to formulate
quantum computation in the Heisenberg picture of quantum theory, whereas the
latter can be used to formulate quantum computation in the Schrödinger picture.
The authors in
[
42
]
use the category
\mathbf{FdOS}
to construct a model of MALL
(Multiplicative Additive Linear Logic) in which the polarised linear logic
duality coincides with the Heisenberg-Schrödinger duality of quantum theory. The
Haagerup tensor, which is used to interpret the seq connective
\triangleleft
of
\mathsf{BV}
,
plays an essential role in this development. The work in
[
42
]
builds on
prior work
[
44
]
on (infinite-dimensional) operator spaces, linear logic
and the Heisenberg-Schrödinger duality where the authors also consider the Haagerup tensor
and discuss its relation to the logic
\mathsf{BV}
and some of its other properties relevant to quantum computation.

7.3
Completely Positive Maps and the Caus[-] Construction

In
[
54
]
, the authors describe a categorical construction, called

{\mathbf{Caus}}[-]
, that produces a new
*
-autonomous category
{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{C}]
with some
additional structure (in particular an extra tensor
<
) from a given compact closed category
\mathbf{C}

satisfying specific conditions. This allows them to construct a
\mathsf{BV}
-category where the tensor
<
represents the connective
\triangleleft
of
\mathsf{BV}
.
There is an evident forgetful functor
U\colon{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{C}]\to\mathbf{C}
which strictly preserves
all of the relevant structure.
The main concrete example is the category

{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb}]]
, where
\mathbf{FHilb}
is the category
of finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces with linear maps as morphisms and
\mathbf{CP}^{*}
is another categorical
construction which gives us a category of completely-positive maps in this case.

If we consider the skeletal subcategory
\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}
whose objects are the Hilbert spaces
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and the morphisms are the complex matrices,
then we can construct a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.

Theorem 27
.

The tuple
\langle{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}],\otimes,<,\mathbb{I},\cdot^{\ast},U\rangle

is a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.

Proof.

Straightforward verification using the results from
[
54
]
and
[
32
]
(see Appendix
D
for more details).
∎

This example also has strong links to quantum theory. The category

\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb}]
is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional von Neumann algebras (equivalently finite-dimensional C*-algebras) and completely positive maps
between them. The category
{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb}]]
then gives a model of higher-order quantum computation (for finite-dimensional systems).

7.4
Probabilistic Coherence Spaces

Our next example is based on a category of finite-dimensional probabilistic coherence spaces
[
24
]
.
The category is described in
[
8
]
and it is
*
-autonomous. The authors in
[
8
]
also define a third tensor product that we use.
We adapt the results from
[
8
]
by considering the skeletal versions
of these constructions.
Let
\mathbf{P_{sk}}
be the category whose objects are pairs
A=(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n},P_{A})
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
P_{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
,
where
\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the set of non-negative reals, such that:

•

P_{A}=P_{A}^{\bot\bot}
, where
P_{A}^{\bot}=\{w\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\mid\langle w,v\rangle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}v_{i}\leq 1\text{ for all }v\in P_{A}\}
;

•

and certain other conditions hold, but we omit them here for brevity.

The homsets
\mathbf{P_{sk}}\big((\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n},P_{A}),(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{m},P_{B})\big)
are given by the

m\times n
matrices with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, such that for all
v\in P_{A}

we have that
Mv\in P_{B}
, where
Mv
stands for the multiplication of the matrix
M
with the vector
v.

We write
\mathbin{\varotimes}
for the tensor product in
\mathbf{P_{sk}}
which can be adapted in an obvious way from the usual definition in
[
15
,
8
,
24
]
.
We write
\oslash
for the (again obvious) adapation of the tensor defined in
[
8
]

to
\mathbf{P_{sk}}
. We note that the action of
\otimes
and
\oslash
on morphisms coincides with the Kronecker product of matrices.
Duals are given by
(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n},P_{A})^{\perp}=(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n},P_{A}^{\perp})
and their action on morphisms is given by transposition of matrices.
Let
\mathbf{Mat}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
be the category whose objects are given by
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and whose homsets

\mathbf{Mat}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+})
are given by the
m\times n
matrices with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}_{+}.
This category
is strict compact closed with all the constructions fully analogous to
\mathbf{FdVect_{sk}}.
We
write
U\colon\mathbf{P_{sk}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Mat}_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}
for the obvious forgetful functor.

Theorem 28
.

The tuple
\langle\mathbf{P_{sk}},\mathbin{\varotimes},\oslash,\mathbb{R}^{+},\cdot^{\bot},U\rangle

is a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.

Proof.

Straightforward verification using the (adapted) results from
[
8
]
.
∎

Probabilistic coherence spaces have been studied in the context of (classical) probabilistic computation
[
20
,
19
]
.
Note that the examples in the previous two subsections also support probabilistic effects.

8
Conclusion and Future Work

In this paper we proposed a notion of
proof identity
for
\mathsf{BV}
, and for this we introduced
atomic flows
for
\mathsf{BV}
. We have shown that the global operation of cut elimination in
\mathsf{BV}
via
splitting
coincides on atomic flows with the local operation of
yanking
. This allowed us to provide a refined notion of
strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category
that is sound for the logic
\mathsf{BV}
, and we provided some concrete non-trivial examples of such categories.

This work brings together two communities: those who study the proof theory of
\mathsf{BV}
and those who study its categorical semantics. This opens up new directions of research on which we elaborate below.

Relation to Pomset Logic.

An independent justification for our choice of proof identity for
\mathsf{BV}
is that it coincides with the one induced by the proof nets of pomset logic. It has been shown in
[
45
,
46
]
, that every
\mathsf{BV}
-proof can be translated into a correct pomset logic proof net. In the cut-free case, this is just the formula in the conclusion together with the axiom links. But the atomic flow of a cut-free
\mathsf{BV}
-derivation with premise
\mathbb{I}
is also just the axiom links on the conclusion. Therefore, two
\mathsf{BV}
proofs have the same atomic flow if and only if they have the same pomset logic proof net. This raises some questions for future research: What is the precise relation between cut elimination in pomset logic proof nets and yanking in atomic flows? And can we have a correctness criterion for
\mathsf{BV}
atomic flows? As shown in
[
45
,
46
]
, the correctness criterion for pomset logic is not suitable for
\mathsf{BV}
.

Strong
\mathsf{BV}
-categories vs
\mathsf{BV}
-categories.

We proved that every strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category is a
\mathsf{BV}
-category in the
sense of
[
8
,
31
]
. We conjecture that the
converse is not true, i.e. there exists a
\mathsf{BV}
-category which cannot be
embedded into a compact closed one in the way that we require. The
embedding of a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category into a strict compact closed category ensures that
we get all the coherence properties for the proof of soundness
(Theorem
23
) and invariance with respect to proof
equivalence (Corollary
24
), for which we
also use results based on atomic flows.
Even though the original definition of a
\mathsf{BV}
-category gives us many coherence diagrams (see Appendix
B
), we do not know if it gives us
all
the ones we need, because the splitting lemma (used for cut elimination / proof normalisation) is difficult to work with categorically, and we doubt that
\mathsf{BV}
-categories give us enough coherence diagrams for atomic flows in relation to Corollary
24
.

We would also like to formulate a more general notion of proof equivalence
compared to the one that we used here (which is based on atomic flows) and then check if the original definition of
\mathsf{BV}
-category gives us enough coherence diagrams for this notion of proof equivalence.
If not, then it would be important to identify the right set of coherence laws for
\mathsf{BV}
-categories and modify the definition of a
\mathsf{BV}
-category accordingly. Another open problem related to this is whether
one can formulate a coherence theorem for
\mathsf{BV}
-categories (in a similar way to Mac Lane’s coherence theorem for monoidal categories
[
41
]
).
The massive complexity discrepancy between pomset logic and
\mathsf{BV}
suggests that this is a non-trivial question.

Yanking without Cut Elimination.

Our main result (
\Cref
thm:YAF) only speaks about derivations with premise
\mathbb{I}
. However, we conjecture that this can be generalized as follows:

Conjecture 29
.

Given a derivation

A

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{SBV}

B

, then there is a derivation

A

\scriptstyle\Phi^{\prime}\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{SBV}

B

, such that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)=\mathsf{Y}\left(\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)\right)
.

This would then also strengthen our second result
\Cref
thm:soundness. We also conjecture that the methods that we need to develop to prove
\Cref
thm:YAF would also help to make progress towards a coherence theorem for
\mathsf{BV}
-categories as discussed above.

Strictness.

\Cref

def:bv-model has a strictness condition on the compact closed category

\mathbf{D}
and on the forgetful functor
U\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
. One reason for
this is that in atomic flows the associativity and unitality of the tensor is “on the nose” and it is not
immediately clear how to relax the strictness condition. It would certainly
require checking many coherence conditions and possibly modifying the proofs in
\Cref
sec:splitting, but we conjecture that
\Cref
thm:soundness can also be extended to the
non-strict case. In particular, we conjecture that the non-strict versions of the concrete categorical models in
\Cref
sec:concrete are sound.
Another reason for the strictness assumption is that it is currently unclear (to us) what is a good notion of a (non-strict)
*
-autonomous functor (compare
[
14
]
and
[
31
]
).
We conjecture that the strictness assumption on the functor
U
can be removed, but this requires performing a more careful analysis of all of the coherence properties that are required to hold in relation to it.

Models of Intuitionistic
\mathsf{BV}
.

Another direction for future work is to investigate suitable categorical models
for Intuitionistic
\mathsf{BV}

[
3
,
2
]
. It would be
interesting to see if natural models can be found by considering categories of
(possibly infinite-dimensional) operator spaces
[
44
,
43
]

where the seq connective
\triangleleft
of
\mathsf{BV}
is interpreted by variants of the
Haagerup tensor (see
[
18
]
).

Appendix A
Omitted Proofs for Section
4

See
9

Proof.

We recall that the standard proof of the splitting lemma proceeds by a case analysis on the last rule of the derivation
\Phi
, and applying the induction hypothesis on the derivation with conclusion the premise of the last rule.
The additional observation here is that each transformation preserves the atomic flow.
This follows from the fact that the only rule in
\mathsf{BV}
whose corresponding atomic flow is not an identity flow is the
\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\downarrow}
rule, and the only case in which it is involved is in the third item above, where the atomic flow is the same modulo the identity over flows given by the fact that string diagrams are considered up-to the equivalences in the right of
\Cref
fig:af, in particular, the ones allowing us to freely ‘slide’ boxes over and under wires.

1.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

(A\mathbin{\varotimes}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K

, then we have three cases:

•

the bottom-most rule is applied to
K
, that is
\Phi={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}
, in which case we can conclude by applying the induction hypothesis to
\Phi^{\prime}
, and then applying the same rule instance
\mathsf{r}
to the resulting derivation, obtaining derivations

\Phi_{K}=\left({{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}

We conclude since the atomic flow of
\mathsf{s}
is an identity flow, therefore we have

(17)

•

the bottom-most rule is applied to
A
or
B
, in which case we can conclude similarly to the previous case by applying the induction hypothesis to the derivation with conclusion the premise of the last rule, and then applying the same rule instance to the resulting derivation.

•

the bottom-most rule is a
\equiv
applied to
A\mathop{\otimes}B
.
In this case, we have that

A=C_{i_{1}}\mathbin{\varotimes}\cdots\mathbin{\varotimes}C_{i_{k}}
and
B=C_{j_{1}}\mathbin{\varotimes}\cdots\mathbin{\varotimes}C_{j_{m}}
for some
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}
such that
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
and that

\Phi={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}

for some
C_{k}^{\prime}\equiv C_{k}
for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
In this case, we repetively apply splitting to the conclusion of
\Phi^{\prime}
, until we get derivations

{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\text{ for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}

And conclude since we can reconstruct

\Phi_{A}={{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}

and

\Phi_{B}={{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}

because we have that

where
K_{A}=K_{i_{1}}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\cdots\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{i_{k}}
and
K_{B}=K_{j_{1}}\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\cdots\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{j_{m}}
;

•

or the bottom-most rule in
\Phi
is a
\mathsf{s}
, that is

\Phi={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}
,
in which case we can apply inductive hypothesis to
\Phi^{\prime}
, obtaining derivations

K_{B}

\scriptstyle\Phi_{K}\;

K

and

\scriptstyle\Phi_{A}\;

A

and

\scriptstyle\Phi_{B}\;

B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K_{B}

and conclude immediately since

\mathsf{AF}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)

because the atomic flow of
\mathsf{s}
is an identity flow.

2.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K

, then we conclude similarly to the previous case.
The only difference is that in this case we have to consider the rule
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}
instead of the rule
\mathsf{s}
, whose atomic flow is not an identity flow, but it is constructed by identities and crossings.

3.

If there is a derivation

\scriptstyle\Phi\;

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{BV}

a\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}K

, then we have two cases:

•

either the last rule is applied to
K
, that is
\Phi={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}
, in which case we can conclude by applying the induction hypothesis to
\Phi^{\prime}
, and then applying the same rule instance
\mathsf{r}
to the resulting derivation, obtaining a derivation
\Phi_{a}=\left({{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)
.

(20)

We can now build the derivation
\Phi^{\ast}
as shown below, whose atomic flow below is such that
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi^{\ast}\right)=\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi\right)
by the equations on atomic flows in
\Cref
eq:qUelimAF1,eq:qUelimAF2 and
\Cref
fig:af.

\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!15}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)\mathbin{\triangleleft}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!15}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)

\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}\;

\left((A\mathbin{\varotimes}C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\mathbin{\varotimes}D)\right)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\left({{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!15}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}\right)

\scriptstyle\Phi^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}\!\left[{(A\mathbin{\varotimes}C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\mathbin{\varotimes}D)}\right]}\;

\mathcal{C}\!\left[{(A\mathbin{\varotimes}C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\mathbin{\varotimes}D)}\right]

\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi_{A}\right)
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi_{C}\right)
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi_{B}\right)
\mathsf{AF}\left(\Phi_{D}\right)

Appendix B
Definition of
\mathsf{BV}
-category

In this appendix we give the definition of a
\mathsf{BV}
-category with negation based on the
approach taken in
[
8
]
with some small adaptations related to presentation.
The presentation and source code for most of the
diagrams is taken from the appendix in
[
42
]
(arxiv version) with some
adaptations on our end.

Definition 30

(Normal Duoidal Structure)
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a category with a monoidal structure

(\mathbf{C},I,\otimes)
and another monoidal structure
(\mathbf{C},J,\mathbin{\triangleleft})
.
We say that

(J,\mathbin{\triangleleft})
is
normal duoidal
to
(I,\otimes)
if
there exists a natural transformation:

w:(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes(C\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)\to(A\otimes C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\otimes D)

called
weak interchange
, together with morphisms

i:I\cong J\qquad\delta_{I}:I\to I\mathbin{\triangleleft}I\qquad\mu_{J}:J\otimes J\to J

which satisfy the following coherence properties:

the weak interchange respects associativity in the sense that the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes(C\mathbin{\triangleleft}D))\otimes(E\mathbin{\triangleleft}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes((C\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)\otimes(E\mathbin{\triangleleft}F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\otimes C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\otimes D))\otimes(E\mathbin{\triangleleft}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes((C\otimes E)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(D\otimes F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\otimes C)\otimes E)\mathbin{\triangleleft}((B\otimes D)\otimes F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes(C\otimes E))\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\otimes(D\otimes F))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\mathbin{\triangleleft}\alpha}

(21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}E)\otimes((B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)\mathbin{\triangleleft}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}(C\mathbin{\triangleleft}E))\otimes(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}(D\mathbin{\triangleleft}F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)\otimes(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D))\mathbin{\triangleleft}(E\otimes F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}((C\mathbin{\triangleleft}E)\otimes(D\mathbin{\triangleleft}F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\otimes B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(C\otimes D))\mathbin{\triangleleft}(E\otimes F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}((C\otimes D)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(E\otimes F))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\otimes\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w\mathbin{\triangleleft}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\triangleleft}w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

(22)

commute; the weak interchange respects unitality in the sense that the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\otimes(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(I\mathbin{\triangleleft}I)\otimes(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(I\otimes A)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(I\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{I}\otimes\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{A}\mathbin{\triangleleft}\lambda_{B}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes(I\mathbin{\triangleleft}I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes I)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\otimes I)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\delta_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A}\mathbin{\triangleleft}\rho_{B}}

(23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\mathbin{\triangleleft}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J\otimes J)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J\mathbin{\triangleleft}A)\otimes(J\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{J}\mathbin{\triangleleft}\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{A}\otimes\lambda_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(J\otimes J)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}J)\otimes(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}J)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\mathbin{\triangleleft}\mu_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}

(24)

commute; we furthermore require that the isomorphism
i
is an isomix map, i.e. the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\mathop{\otimes}J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J\mathop{\otimes}I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\mathop{\otimes}J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}\mathop{\otimes}\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes i^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes;
we also require that
(I,i,\delta_{I})
is a comonoid object with respect to the
(\mathcal{C},J,\triangleleft)
monoidal structure
and we require that
(J,i^{-1},\mu_{J})
is a monoid object with respect to the
(\mathcal{C},I,\otimes)
monoidal structure.

Definition 31
.

A
\mathsf{BV}
-category with negation
is a
*
-autonomous category

{(\mathbf{C},I,\otimes,\multimap)}
with an additional monoidal
structure

(\mathbf{C},J,\mathbin{\triangleleft})
, such that

•

(\mathbf{C},J,\mathbin{\triangleleft})
is
normal duoidal
to
(\mathbf{C},I,\otimes)
and we write

w\colon(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes(C\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)\to(A\otimes C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\otimes D)

for the weak interchange natural transformation; we also require that

w
commutes with the symmetry of
\otimes
in the sense that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)\otimes(C\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)\otimes(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes C)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(B\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\otimes A)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(D\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(25)

•

(\mathbf{C},\perp,\mathbin{\bindnasrepma})
is
normal duoidal
to
(\mathbf{C},J,\mathbin{\triangleleft})
and we write

w^{\prime}\colon(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D)\to(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)

for the weak interchange natural transformation; we also require that

w^{\prime}
commutes with the symmetry of
\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}
in the sense that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}B)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}A)\mathbin{\triangleleft}(D\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\mathbin{\triangleleft}D)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(A\mathbin{\triangleleft}C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(26)

•

We also require that the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\triangleleft B)\mathop{\otimes}((C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}E)\triangleleft(D\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\mathop{\otimes}(C\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}E))\triangleleft(B\mathop{\otimes}(D\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\triangleleft B)\mathop{\otimes}((C\triangleleft D)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(E\triangleleft F))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\mathop{\otimes}C)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}E)\triangleleft((B\mathop{\otimes}D)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\triangleleft B)\mathop{\otimes}(C\triangleleft D))\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(E\triangleleft F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\mathop{\otimes}C)\triangleleft(B\mathop{\otimes}D))\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}(E\triangleleft F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\triangleleft a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}\mathrm{id}}

(27)

and its symmetric dual commute. Here we write

a_{ABC}\colon A\mathop{\otimes}(B\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C)\to(A\mathop{\otimes}B)\mathbin{\bindnasrepma}C

for the canonical morphism that may be defined via the
*
-autonomous structure.

This definition implies that the
*
-autonomous structure of
\mathbf{C}
is isomix.

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
21

Lemma 21
.

For a

\mathsf{SBV}
-derivation

\Phi={{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{{{}}}{\fcolorbox{currentcolor!0}{currentcolor!5}{$\vcenter{\copy\derboxone}$}}}
, the following diagram commutes (in
\mathbf{D}
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U{\left\llbracket A\right\rrbracket}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\llparenthesis A\rrparenthesis}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U{\left\llbracket B\right\rrbracket}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\llparenthesis B\rrparenthesis}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U{\left\llbracket\Phi\right\rrbracket}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\llparenthesis\Phi\rrparenthesis}}

(28)

Proof.

This follows by induction on
\Phi
. The two most interesting cases are
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}
. The case for
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\downarrow}
follows immediately from the definition of a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.
The case for
\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}
follows easily, because
U{\left\llbracket\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}\right\rrbracket}=U(\cong)\circ U({\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket}^{\perp})\circ U(\cong)=\mathrm{id}\circ(\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma^{\perp}\boxtimes\mathrm{id})\circ\mathrm{id}=\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\sigma\boxtimes\mathrm{id}={\llparenthesis\mathsf{q}\mathord{\uparrow}\rrparenthesis}

where we used the fact that
U
strictly preserves all the relevant structure and maps the two unnamed isomorphisms to identities in
\mathbf{D}
.

The remaining cases are straightforward and follow easily from standard results about
*
-autonomous and compact closed categories. We illustrate this by considering the

\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}
rule.

In order to prove this, recall that a compact closed category
\mathbf{D}
is symmetric monoidal closed with internal hom
A\multimap B=B\otimes A^{\bot}
and evaluation morphism

\mathrm{ev}^{\prime}_{A,B}\colon(A\multimap B)\otimes A\to B

defined by

\hfill\mathrm{ev}^{\prime}_{A,B}=\left((A\multimap B)\otimes A=B\otimes A^{\bot}\otimes A\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\sigma}B\otimes A\otimes A^{\bot}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\epsilon_{A}}B\right)\qquad,\hfill

where
\epsilon_{A}\colon A\otimes A^{\bot}\to\mathbb{I}
is the counit of the compact closed structure.
By definition, we have that

\hfill{\left\llbracket{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to25.0996pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.74423pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 3.19444pt\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}\kern 3.19444pt$}}\kern 10.74423pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}\otimes{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\xrightarrow{\sigma}{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}^{\bot}\otimes{\left\llbracket a\right\rrbracket}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}}\mathbb{I}\qquad.\hfill

Applying
U
to this definition, we get

\displaystyle U(\mathrm{ev})\circ U(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ev}^{\prime}\circ\sigma

(
U
strictly preserves
*
-autonomous structure)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{I}}\otimes\epsilon)\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{I}}\otimes\sigma)\circ\sigma

(Definition)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\circ\sigma\circ\sigma

(
\mathbf{D}
is strict monoidal)

\displaystyle=\epsilon

(Property of symmetry)

\displaystyle=\left(\!\left|{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 14.97218pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a\mathop{\otimes}a^{\bot}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\mathsf{ai}\mathord{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to25.0996pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 10.74423pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 3.19444pt\hbox{$\mathbb{I}$}\kern 3.19444pt$}}\kern 10.74423pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\right|\!\right)

(Definition)

∎

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
27

See
27

Proof.

The category
\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}
is compact closed, because it is categorically equivalent to
\mathbf{Mat}_{\mathbb{C}}
from
[
32
]

which is shown to be compact closed in
[
32
]
. Moreover, it is easy to see that it is
strict
compact closed by examining the
relevant data in
[
32
]
.
Furthermore, it is straightforward to see that it is
dagger
compact closed (with dagger given by the conjugate transpose of matrices)
because it is the skeletal subcategory of
\mathbf{FHilb}

which is well-known to be dagger compact closed
[
32
]
.
The
\mathbf{CP}^{*}[-]
construction (see
[
32
, Chapter 7]
) preserves dagger compact closure
[
32
, Proposition 7.26]
, so it follows that

\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]
is also dagger compact closed. Furthermore,
\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]
is a strict symmetric monoidal category

[
32
, Proposition 7.22]
. The double dual isomorphism in
\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]
is the identity, which can be easily proven using
[
32
, Proposition 7.24]
.
Moreover, we also have that
(A\boxtimes B)^{\perp}=A^{\perp}\boxtimes B^{\perp}
in
\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]
which can be seen by using
[
32
, Proposition 7.24]
and
[
32
, Proposition 7.22]
.
It follows that
\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]
is a strict compact closed category. Note that since
\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}\simeq\mathbf{FHilb}
are categorically equivalent (the former is the skeleton of the latter),
the results from
[
54
]
may be readily reused for the category
\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}
as well. It follows that
{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]]
is a
\mathsf{BV}
-category with negation (in the original sense)
which gives us Definition
17
(1.) – (4.). Note that the authors in
[
54
]

have proven that the
\kappa
isomorphism in this category is the identity, i.e.
A^{\perp}\triangleleft B^{\perp}=(A\triangleleft B)^{\perp}
.
Examining the constructions in
[
54
]
we see that the forgetful functor
U\colon{\mathbf{Caus}}[\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]]\to\mathbf{CP}^{*}[\mathbf{FHilb_{sk}}]
preserves all the required structure strictly
and satisfies Definition
17
(5.) thus giving us a strong
\mathsf{BV}
-category.
∎
